Covering the body and blocking the window protects the dignity of the deceased while the paperwork is completed. Moving the body first leaves it exposed during the carry and delays the privacy step.
  93. The correct answer is B (Wait for the coroner's decision before moving the body.): When a coroner is involved, the coroner controls the movement of the body and the team waits for that direction. Family authority to arrange the funeral does not override the coroner's role in the investigation. Beginning the removal, even to the hospital morgue, can interfere with the coroner's work.
  94. The correct answer is C (Call the funeral home for the extra protective equipment and follow special precautions.): The competency profile requires the licensee to identify and follow special requirements for cases involving infections, diseases and traumatic conditions. The team should obtain the right barriers and use the precautions the case requires before handling the body. Starting with gloves alone, sending the daughter away or waiting on the family does not address the hazard.
  95. The correct answer is D (Use a replacement pouch that closes and secures the body.): The body must be covered and secure during movement, so a pouch that will not close is replaced before the transfer continues. Carrying the body uncovered or with the pouch open exposes it to view and risks a shift on the cot. A family blanket or a tied zipper does not provide the same secure covering.
  96. The correct answer is B (Arrange for a qualified person to remove the pacemaker before cremation.): Section 31(2) prohibits a crematorium operator from permitting cremation of a body with a pacemaker, and section 52 limits who may be engaged to remove it to a Funeral Director Class 1 acting for a Class 1 operator or a legally qualified medical practitioner. Family consent does not change that rule. The body waits until a qualified person removes the device.
  97. The correct answer is A (Move the body into the holding room before helping with the caskets.): The licensee's duty to keep the body safe and secure applies while the body is in the funeral home's possession or control. Moving the body into the holding room places it in the licensed, protected space designed for it. A locked vehicle or a volunteer watcher does not provide the same secure and dignified holding.
  98. The correct answer is D (Resolve the discrepancy with the sending home before moving the body.): The chapter states that any discrepancy is resolved with the sending funeral home before anything else happens. Accepting an explanation, altering the label or relying on the family's confirmation leaves the identification question unresolved. The viewing schedule can be adjusted once identity is confirmed.
  99. The correct answer is C (Cover the body, keep the cot moving and answer the roommate briefly and kindly.): The chapter directs the licensee to cover the body during movement, keep doors closed and speak about the deceased by name. A brief, kind answer respects the roommate without turning the removal into a public event. Stopping for a detailed description or clearing the hallway adds attention and delay.
  100. The correct answer is A (Do not cremate unless the shorter period is shown to be safe.): Section 31(2.1) permits cremation of a body with a radioactive implant only if at least two years have passed since the implant, or if a shorter time has passed and it is safe to cremate the body. Six months is less than two years, so the operator needs confirmation that cremation is safe. The funeral home's assurance alone does not satisfy the condition.
  101. The correct answer is B (Setting the features, which arranges the mouth and the eyes for viewing): Setting the features is the step that positions and fixes the mouth and the eyes so the face rests naturally. Restoration rebuilds damaged tissue with materials such as wax, and it sits outside the non-embalming scope. Casketing and disinfection are separate steps that happen at other points in the sequence. On a body that will not be embalmed, the methods used to hold the features are temporary because the tissues dry and change.
  102. The correct answer is B (The cause of death along with the person's own medical history): Intrinsic factors come from within the body, and the cause of death, medical treatments, wounds and the person's build all sit in that group. The room temperature, the hours since death and the position of the body come from outside the body, so they are extrinsic factors. Separating the two groups helps the licensee predict how the body will behave during preparation. Oedema, emaciation and infection are further examples of intrinsic factors.
  103. The correct answer is D (The warmth of the room where the body was stored): Extrinsic factors come from outside the body, and the temperature of the room or refrigerator, the time since death, the position of the body and any chemicals present all belong to that group. The person's build, a treated wound and an infection inside the body are intrinsic factors. Both groups are weighed before the preparation plan is chosen. The time since death matters because it affects how quickly the tissues change.
  104. The correct answer is C (The interior must not be visible from a public area when the doors are closed): Section 47 of Ontario Regulation 30/11 requires that the interior of a holding room not be visible from a public area when the doors are closed. The room must also be easy to clean, sanitize and disinfect, and it must hold the supplies needed to prepare bodies. A light wall colour is a practical choice, but it is not a requirement of the section. Locating the room beside an office has no basis in the regulation.
  105. The correct answer is A (Mechanical ventilation fitted to run directly to the outside of the building): Section 47 of Ontario Regulation 30/11 lists mechanical ventilation directly to the outside as required equipment in a holding room. The same section requires running water with a device to prevent back-flow, a supply of personal protective equipment, and materials for cleaning and disinfecting. A portable purifier, an open window or a ceiling fan does not meet the wording of the requirement. The rule exists because air from a holding room should not be recirculated through the building.
  106. The correct answer is C (Running water with a device that prevents the back-flow of water): Section 47 of Ontario Regulation 30/11 requires a holding room to have a supply of running water with a device to prevent back-flow. The same section requires materials for cleaning and disinfecting, personal protective equipment for each person handling a body, and mechanical ventilation to the outside. A stored bottled supply or a basin drained by hand does not satisfy the running water requirement. Back-flow prevention protects both the water supply and the people working in the room.
  107. The correct answer is B (Washing the body, including applying a disinfectant externally, and setting the features): Section 36 of Ontario Regulation 30/11 allows a Transfer Service Operator Class 1 to place bodies in caskets and to care for or prepare them by washing, including the external application of a disinfectant, and by setting the features. The same subsection bars the operator from selling or providing arterial embalming or restoration. Subsection 36 (5) requires a funeral director to perform the permitted preparation work. Cavity embalming and hypodermic injection are embalming procedures and sit outside this licence.
  108. The correct answer is D (Providing the service of arterially embalming a dead human body for a family): Section 40 of Ontario Regulation 30/11 provides that a Funeral Director Class 2 shall not provide the services of arterially embalming or restoring dead human bodies. The same section allows the licensee to offer to sell those services only while acting on behalf of an operator that holds a Funeral Establishment Operator Class 1 licence. Washing, disinfecting, setting the features and casketing all sit inside the Class 2 scope. Offering or providing arterial embalming outside those limits is a breach of the licence condition.
  109. The correct answer is A (Only while acting for an operator holding a Funeral Establishment Operator Class 1 licence): Section 40 of Ontario Regulation 30/11 permits a Funeral Director Class 2 to offer to sell or sell arterial embalming or restoration services only when acting on behalf of an operator that holds a Funeral Establishment Operator Class 1 licence. The same section forbids the licensee from providing those services at all. Family consent, a municipal business licence or extra training does not change the class of licence involved. The class of the operator licence is what determines whether the service may be sold.
  110. The correct answer is A (Removing personal effects, placing the body in a casket and transporting it): Section 37 of Ontario Regulation 30/11 states that no employee of a Transfer Service Operator Class 2 is to have contact with the dead human body except to remove personal effects, place the body in a casket and transport the body. Washing, disinfecting, setting the features and dressing are preparation steps and sit outside that list. A Transfer Service Operator Class 1 may provide some of those steps under section 36, and a funeral director must perform them. A Class 2 operator that prepares a body would be working outside its licence conditions.
  111. The correct answer is D (It shall not be transported later than 72 hours after removal from refrigeration): Section 35 of Ontario Regulation 30/11 states that a transfer service operator shall not transport a dead human body that has not been arterially embalmed later than 72 hours after the death or the removal of the body from refrigeration. The clock runs from the death or from the time the body left refrigeration, whichever applies. Family consent, cemetery approval and the presence of a funeral director do not extend the time. A body that must travel later is expected to be arterially embalmed first.
  112. The correct answer is A (Where the item came from, who received it, and its date and condition): A personal effects record protects both the family and the funeral home, so it should list each item received, where it came from, who received it, the date and time, and the condition of the item. Valuables are stored securely and are not left in an unlocked area. The retail value and the insurer are not part of the standard record, and the weight of an item adds nothing useful. If a family reports a missing item, the record is the first document to check.
  113. The correct answer is B (The size of the casket and the material and colour of the interior): Section 44 of Ontario Regulation 30/11 requires casket information to include the price, a colour image, the size, the material and colour of the interior, the material, colour, finish and thickness of the exterior, and the brand and model name and number. The information must be enough for a purchaser to identify the casket with certainty. The weight of a casket, the delivery date, the warranty and the supplier name are not required by the section. The same section also requires a display model for each type of casket named in section 43.
  114. The correct answer is C (The tissues will dry and change, so the placement will not hold for long): On a body that will not be embalmed, the tissues dry and change after death, so the mouth and eye placements cannot be expected to hold for a long period. Cotton rolls, mouth formers and adhesives are chosen according to the condition of the body and the length of the viewing. Nothing in the regulation sets a 24-hour limit for removing setting materials. Family members are not expected to remove any materials themselves.
  115. The correct answer is D (At the time of the work rather than from memory at a later date): The preparation report is a legal record, so it is written at the time of the work and not rebuilt from memory days later. It should show the instructions received, the preparation performed, the products used, any tissue damage or unusual condition, the personal effects handled and the person who did the work. Keeping the report factual and unemotional protects everyone involved if a question arises later. A report written in advance would record a plan rather than what actually happened.
  116. The correct answer is D (Verify the directions directly with the person who has the authority to give them): The competency profile asks the licensee to verify, in a respectful way, the authorization to perform any preparation with the person who made the arrangements or the legal representative directly. Verification is a conversation rather than a glance at a form, so the key points are confirmed out loud. Payment, a price list or a fast start does not replace that step. Beginning work before the direction is confirmed risks doing something the family refused.
  117. The correct answer is A (Stop and confirm the new instruction in writing before any further work continues): The chapter directs that if a family changes its mind after preparation has begun, the licensee stops and confirms the new instruction in writing before continuing. Continuing with the old plan, even with a note in the file, would carry out a direction the family has withdrawn. Asking for a second arrangement conference delays the work without solving the problem. Recording who authorized the change and when is part of the same step.
  118. The correct answer is B (Leave the face without cosmetics and check the skin is clean): The chapter states that when a family asks for no cosmetics at all, that instruction is followed exactly. Adding powder, lip colour or any other product would go against a direction the family put in writing. Checking that the skin is clean is part of bathing and grooming and does not conflict with the instruction. Recording the instruction and the fact that it was followed belongs in the case file.
  119. The correct answer is C (Follow the written direction and record that the step was carried out): The competency profile asks the licensee to identify and follow preparation instructions, including cultural, religious and personal requirements. Recorded directions are carried out as they were given, and the work is documented in the case file. A standard house routine is not a substitute for a specific religious direction. Bathing a body in a particular way sits inside the non-embalming scope, so the request can be met.
  120. The correct answer is A (Stop, correct the problem, then confirm the casket is dry before casketing): The chapter's worked example states that the correct response is to stop and fix the problem before the body is placed inside. A damp interior can affect the body and the appearance of the casket, and the damage becomes harder to correct once the body is in place. Extra bedding hides the symptom without removing the moisture. Problems found before the family arrives are ordinary work, while problems found in front of them disrupt the goodbye.
  121. The correct answer is D (Set the incorrect casket aside and obtain the model the family selected): The competency profile requires the licensee to obtain and inspect the selected casket or container, so the model in the room must be the model the family chose. If the casket must be changed, the family is told what happened and what will be provided instead. Substituting a different casket and adjusting the price later does not meet that requirement. The inspection step exists to catch this problem before the body is placed inside.
  122. The correct answer is C (Contact the supplier or choose a replacement casket, then tell the family): The chapter states that a scratch on the lid will be visible to the family, and the correct response is to contact the supplier or select a replacement casket and confirm the model the family chose before continuing. The family is told what happened and what will be provided instead. Covering the area or treating the mark as normal places the funeral home's convenience ahead of the family's experience. A damaged casket is not the casket the family selected.
  123. The correct answer is B (Tell the family early and discuss alterations or a different outfit): The chapter directs the licensee to confirm that the clothing fits, and if it does not, to tell the family early and discuss options such as alterations or a different outfit. Forcing the jacket closed or hiding the gap changes the appearance the family will see. Substituting a garment from the funeral home's cupboard without asking does not follow the family's direction. The conversation is easier before the viewing than after it.
  124. The correct answer is A (Cover the wound with a suitable dressing before the clothing goes on): The chapter states that wounds, intravenous sites, drains and stomas are handled carefully, covered with a suitable dressing, and checked so the dressing does not show through the clothing. Where leakage is likely, the plan is made before dressing rather than after an accident. Waiting for leakage to appear leaves the clothing and the casket interior at risk. Cosmetics hide colour on the skin and do nothing about fluid from a wound.
  125. The correct answer is D (Use the photograph as a guide and return it once styling ends): The chapter directs the licensee to style the hair close to the way the person wore it, using a photograph when the family provides one. It also states that the photograph is kept with the case record and returned afterward. Placing an original family photograph in the casket would remove it from the family permanently. Scanning a copy is helpful, but the original still belongs back with the family.
  126. The correct answer is B (Remove the devices and equipment that need to come off before the bathing): The chapter states that the licensee unshrouds the body, confirms the identification, covers the genitals, and removes all devices or equipment that need to be removed before bathing or dressing. Leaving a line in place or cutting it close to the skin creates a safety risk and does not follow that sequence. Asking the facility to come back later delays the preparation. Work that involves the body is done by the funeral home once the body is in its care.
  127. The correct answer is C (Adjust the dressing or the sleeve so that the dressing does not show): The chapter asks the licensee to cover wounds with a suitable dressing and to check that the dressing does not show through the clothing. Adjusting the dressing or the sleeve solves the problem while keeping the wound covered. Removing the dressing to improve the look of the shirt leaves the wound exposed. Telling the family their shirt is unsuitable does not address the dressing itself.
  128. The correct answer is D (Step back and check the face from the distance the family will view it): The chapter notes that a face which looks correct at two feet may look odd from six feet away, where most visitors stand. The licensee therefore checks the result from the family's viewing distance rather than from close up. A photograph taken close to the face does not show what visitors will see. Checking after the lid is closed removes the chance to make an adjustment.
  129. The correct answer is A (Moisten the dry areas with a suitable lubricant or a light ointment): The chapter directs the licensee to moisten dry eyes and lips with a suitable lubricant or ointment while setting the features. Dry tissue keeps the eyelids from meeting smoothly, so moisturising helps the lids close naturally. Cosmetics cover the surface without restoring moisture and can make dry tissue look worse. Leaving the eyes open or covering them with a cloth is not part of the feature setting sequence.
  130. The correct answer is C (Support the jaw so that the mouth and the profile sit correctly): The chapter states that the licensee supports the jaw if it has fallen back and that the mouth is closed gently so the expression does not look forced. Reopening the mouth and forcing a larger former in place works against that direction. Adjusting the lighting or asking the family about the person's appearance does not correct the profile. Feature setting is judged against the family's viewing distance and their photograph.
  131. The correct answer is B (Place the items as directed and record them in the case file): The chapter states that personal effects are added only as directed by the legal representative, and that items placed with the body should be recorded because the family may later ask what went in. A book and a photograph are ordinary items and there is no rule limiting casket items to jewellery. Recording only a photograph of the items does not create the written list the chapter describes. The record protects the funeral home if a question comes up later.
  132. The correct answer is D (Document the wedding ring and return it promptly and safely to the family): The chapter states that items which cannot be placed, such as jewellery the family wants returned, are documented and returned promptly and safely. The family has asked for the ring back rather than for it to be worn, so leaving it on the body or storing it in the casket ignores that direction. Keeping the ring in the preparation room would leave a valuable item in an uncontrolled space. The costume ring, not the wedding ring, is worn for the viewing.
  133. The correct answer is A (The identification tags attached to the body must be verified first): The competency profile requires the licensee to verify the identification tags affixed to the body before carrying out any procedures. The chapter places that check at the start of unshrouding and undressing, before bathing or dressing begins. A burial permit, a clothing decision and a casket measurement are all separate matters and none of them identifies the person on the table. Mixing up two bodies is the single most serious risk in a preparation room.
  134. The correct answer is B (A Funeral Director Class 1 acting for a Class 1 operator): Section 52 of Ontario Regulation 30/11 permits a pacemaker or radioactive implant to be removed from a dead human body by a person who holds a Funeral Director Class 1 licence and is acting on behalf of an operator holding a Funeral Establishment Operator Class 1 or Transfer Service Operator Class 1 licence, or by a legally qualified medical practitioner. An unlicensed assistant, a Class 2 funeral director and a Class 2 transfer service employee cannot do the removal. A crematorium operator must not permit cremation while a pacemaker or radioactive implant remains in the body.
  135. The correct answer is B (Follow the family's instruction and record where the watch is stored): The chapter directs the licensee to follow the family's instruction about when an item is returned and to record it. Some families want valuables back immediately, and others want them held until after the service. Placing a family watch in the casket or handing it to whoever visits next does not follow that direction. Stored valuables are kept in a secure place rather than an unlocked area.
  136. The correct answer is C (The face, hands, clothing, hair, the casket interior and the lighting): The chapter lists the face, the hands, the clothing, the hair, the casket interior and the lighting as the points to check in the final inspection before the family sees the body. The same standard asks the licensee to monitor the body until the time of disposition. Room temperature, the guest book, the flower cards and the parking arrangements belong to the service arrangements rather than to the body. The casket lid and handles were checked when the casket was inspected and centred.
  137. The correct answer is D (Monitor the body until the time of disposition and address any changes): The competency profile requires the licensee to perform a final inspection and to monitor the body until the time of disposition. Changes such as movement of the features, drainage or discolouration are dealt with as they appear rather than left for the family to see. Re-setting features or adding cosmetics on a fixed twice-daily schedule is not what the standard asks for. Moving the body each evening is not required and adds handling the body does not need.
  138. The correct answer is C (Cover the body and speak about the deceased using their name): The chapter states that dignity is maintained at every step, including covering the body during movement and speaking about the deceased by name. Working quietly to avoid notice is not the same as acting with respect, and leaving a body uncovered during a move removes that dignity. Sending the family out of the room does not address how the body is handled. The same standard applies to two people working together on a transfer.
  139. The correct answer is A (Confirm the identification and cover the genitals before continuing further): The competency profile describes unshrouding the body in a dignified manner, with accurate identification attached and the genitals covered. Those steps come first, before undressing and before any bathing or disinfecting. Removing clothing too early or washing the face before the body is on the table gets the sequence out of order. Working alone with a body is avoided where the task or the space makes a partner safer.
  140. The correct answer is A (Take the report seriously and check the personal effects record): The chapter states that if a family tells the licensee an item is missing, the report is treated seriously and the personal effects record is checked. The record lists each item received, where it came from, who received it, the date and time, and the condition of the item. There is no rule that unclaimed items are discarded after two days, and the funeral home is responsible for items it received. Telling the family to go to the police or refusing responsibility does not address the record.
  141. The correct answer is C (Tell the family early and offer options such as a shorter viewing): The chapter states that where the licensee believes the plan will not produce the result the family expects, the family is told early and offered alternatives such as a shorter viewing, a closed casket or reconsidering embalming. Making the decision alone removes the family's right to choose. Hoping that nobody notices, or covering changes with more cosmetics each day, hides a problem the family will eventually see. Early honesty protects the family's expectations and the funeral home's reputation.
  142. The correct answer is D (Explain that the licence does not permit the procedure and discuss options): Section 40 of Ontario Regulation 30/11 states that a Funeral Director Class 2 shall not provide the services of arterially embalming or restoring dead human bodies. No form of family consent changes a licence condition, and recording the work as cosmetics would be a false record. Asking an unlicensed assistant to do the injection compounds the breach. The licensee explains the limit and discusses a plan that fits the licence, such as a shorter viewing or a closed casket.
  143. The correct answer is A (That work is outside the licence, which allows contact only for listed tasks): Section 37 of Ontario Regulation 30/11 states that no employee of a Transfer Service Operator Class 2 is to have contact with the dead human body except to remove personal effects, place the body in a casket and transport the body. Washing and dressing are preparation steps, so they fall outside that licence no matter who supervises or what the family signs. Charging for the work makes the breach worse because it is both outside the licence and a billed service the operator cannot sell. A Transfer Service Operator Class 1 may provide some preparation through a funeral director.
  144. The correct answer is B (Discuss the findings with the family and explain what can be offered): A case analysis exists to answer whether the plan the family chose can produce the result they expect, and the answer here is no. Refrigeration slows change but does not restore a presentable appearance for a public viewing, and a stronger disinfectant does not either. Cancelling without a conversation takes the decision away from the family. The licensee explains the condition and offers realistic alternatives, such as a private viewing, a closed casket or a shorter time.
  145. The correct answer is D (The operator's current licence does not authorize the sale of arterial embalming): Section 34 of Ontario Regulation 30/11 states that a Funeral Establishment Operator Class 2 shall not offer to sell, sell or provide a transfer service or any funeral services other than maintaining premises for visitation. Arterial embalming can only be offered by an operator holding a Funeral Establishment Operator Class 1 licence, and section 39 attaches the same requirement to a Funeral Director Class 1. Advertising the service would be an offer to sell it. The operator would need the Class 1 licence, not just a qualified staff member, before the service could be sold.
  146. The correct answer is A (Discuss the risks with the family and then follow their documented direction): Personal effects are added as directed by the legal representative, so a family request to place items in the casket is theirs to make. The licensee raises the practical concerns, such as the card and the keys having no use to the deceased, and then follows the direction that is recorded. Refusing outright, or quietly removing one item, overrides the family's instruction. Whatever goes in is written on the personal effects record so the family can be told later what was placed.
  147. The correct answer is C (Follow the recorded direction and ask the legal representative to settle the matter): The licensee takes direction from the legal representative, and the recorded instruction is the one to follow when two family members disagree. Handing the watch to whoever asks first hands the funeral home's authority to the loudest voice in the room. Sealing the watch in a safe or leaving it on the body also changes the recorded direction. The representative resolves the disagreement between the family members, and the file records what was decided.
  148. The correct answer is B (Assess each body separately and plan the preparation for its condition): The case analysis weighs the intrinsic and extrinsic factors of each body, and the time at room temperature is one of the strongest extrinsic factors. A body that stayed warm will change faster and may need a different plan, even for the same one-hour viewing. Running one identical routine for both bodies ignores those differences. Treating families equally means giving each case the attention it needs, not using the same steps for both.
  149. The correct answer is D (Obtain a correctly sized casket and inform the family of the change): The inspection step exists so the casket matches the family's selection and fits the body before the placement happens. Angling the body, bending the knees or stripping out bedding damages the appearance and leaves the arrangement visibly wrong. The correct response is to obtain the right size and tell the family what happened and what will be provided instead. Informing the family matters because a change of casket affects what they believed they purchased.
  150. The correct answer is C (Establish which person holds the authority and confirm the instruction with them): The competency profile requires the licensee to verify the authorization to perform any preparation with the person who made the arrangements or the legal representative directly. When the file does not show who that is, the licensee settles the question before touching the body. Following whichever sibling speaks first, or doing nothing until the viewing begins, leaves the work in limbo and risks a conflict at the door. Once the direction is clear and recorded, the cosmetic step follows it exactly.
  151. The correct answer is C (The legal representative of the deceased or the arranging funeral director.): The competency profile requires the licensee to verify permission to embalm with the funeral director who made the arrangements or with the legal representative of the deceased directly. The check is made in a respectful manner before any preparation begins. A physician, a hospital administrator or a cemetery operator may be involved in the death or the disposition, but none of them gives this permission.
  152. The correct answer is A (The tags attached to the body identify the person named in the file.): The competency profile requires the licensee to verify the identification tags affixed to the body before embalming, so the body on the table is the body named in the authorization. The tag check is repeated before arterial injection begins. The other items belong to the office side of the arrangement and do not identify the body being prepared.
  153. The correct answer is D (Honour the recorded requirement and plan a preparation that respects it.): The competency profile requires the licensee to identify and adhere to any cultural, religious or personal requirements for embalming, and the arranging funeral director has already recorded this one. Injecting the body against the family's stated requirement damages the trust they placed in the funeral home. If the viewing cannot be provided as the family wishes, the licensee explains the limits and discusses the options instead of deciding alone.
  154. The correct answer is B (Confirm the agreed plan with the arranging funeral director first.): The competency profile requires effective and accurate communication between the arranging funeral director and the funeral director who performs the embalming, so the two of them confirm the plan before the work begins. A written contract may not carry every instruction that the family gave during the conference. Repeating the whole conference with the family would be intrusive and unnecessary.
  155. The correct answer is A (Confirm with the legal representative that restoration is also permitted.): Permission to embalm covers the embalming, and restoration is a separate service that must also be authorized. The competency profile requires the licensee to verify permission to embalm or restore the body, so the licensee asks the legal representative before rebuilding the damaged area. A licensee who restores without permission has gone beyond what the family approved, even when the intent was to help.
  156. The correct answer is C (Hold the case and clarify who has the right to decide.): The licensee must verify permission with the person who holds the lawful right to decide, and a dispute between relatives does not settle that question. Preparing the body before the matter is clarified could mean acting without permission, which cannot be undone. Once the proper person is identified, the licensee records the decision and proceeds.
  157. The correct answer is B (It is the grams of formaldehyde gas in 100 millilitres of fluid.): The index of a fluid is the number of grams of formaldehyde gas dissolved in 100 millilitres of the fluid, expressed as a percentage. A 22 index fluid therefore holds about 22 per cent formaldehyde gas by weight. The index describes the concentrate in the bottle and does not by itself say how much water the licensee will add.
  158. The correct answer is D (The index describes the concentrate and the strength describes the diluted solution.): The index tells the licensee how much formaldehyde gas is in the concentrated product, while the strength describes the percentage of formaldehyde in the diluted solution that is injected. The same bottle can produce solutions of different strengths, depending on how much water is added. The strength of the solution is what the tissues actually receive.
  159. The correct answer is A (The mixture produces a solution of about 4 per cent formaldehyde.): Twenty index fluid mixed one part to four parts water gives a solution of about 4 per cent formaldehyde, because the index is divided by the total number of parts in the mixture. The same product mixed one part to nine parts water gives about 2 per cent. The stronger mix here fits a case that needs more preserving power than a routine body.
  160. The correct answer is C (One part of fluid mixed with nineteen parts of water.): Twenty index fluid mixed one part to nineteen parts water produces a solution of about 1 per cent formaldehyde. A weaker solution like this one protects tissue that is thin and dry and suits a body that will be viewed for a short time. The index and the total number of parts in the mixture set the strength.
  161. The correct answer is B (The 30 index product, because it carries more formaldehyde.): At the same mixing ratio, the fluid with the higher index puts more formaldehyde into the solution, so the 30 index product gives the stronger mix. A higher index product is chosen when a case needs more preserving power. The licensee still checks the case analysis before deciding what strength the body needs.
  162. The correct answer is D (About fourteen litres of water for each litre of fluid.): Thirty index fluid mixed one part to fourteen parts water produces a solution of about 2 per cent formaldehyde, because the index is divided by the fifteen total parts. The same ratio with a 20 index fluid would give a weaker result. The licensee checks the arithmetic before the fluid is poured.
  163. The correct answer is A (About 1.2 litres of concentrate for the twelve litre batch.): The mix is one part fluid to nine parts water, so the fluid is one tenth of the total volume. One tenth of twelve litres is about 1.2 litres of concentrate, and the rest of the batch is water. The plan is built from the index of the fluid and the strength the case needs.
  164. The correct answer is C (A stronger solution than the routine mix, with thorough drainage.): A body that died of an infectious illness and is already showing change needs a stronger arterial solution, because the solution must overcome active organisms and early tissue breakdown. Thorough drainage and cavity treatment are part of the same plan. Extra water, a weaker mix or the routine mix would leave the body under-preserved.
  165. The correct answer is D (Mix the strength the body needs rather than the routine suggestion.): The dilution printed on the label is a starting point, while the case analysis decides the strength that a particular body needs. The licensee mixes to that strength and records the reason for the choice. Treating one dilution as a rule for every case would leave some bodies under-preserved and others over-treated.
  166. The correct answer is B (It fixes tissue proteins and it destroys organisms on contact.): Formaldehyde fixes tissue proteins, which is the preservative action, and it also destroys organisms, which is the disinfecting action. Those two jobs explain why the chemical forms the base of most arterial fluids. Humectants, dyes, buffers, anticoagulants and water conditioners each do a different job in the same bottle.
  167. The correct answer is A (Formaldehyde, glutaraldehyde and phenol are the preservative chemicals.): Formaldehyde, glutaraldehyde and phenol fix tissue proteins and stop bacterial growth, which makes them the preservatives in the bottle. Glycerine and sorbitol are humectants that hold moisture in the tissue, while salts such as borax and citrate serve other functions in the mixture. A water conditioner and a dye each do a different job and do not preserve tissue.
  168. The correct answer is C (A buffering agent, because the preservative works best near a set acidity.): Buffers control the acidity of the solution so that the preservative can work properly, which is why a poorly buffered mixture can leave tissue under-treated. The licensee adds the buffering product the case needs and checks the result as the injection proceeds. Colour, moisture and surface tension are handled by other ingredients.
  169. The correct answer is D (An anticoagulant, which slows the clotting that blocks small vessels.): Anticoagulants slow the clotting that can block small vessels and prevent the solution from reaching parts of the body. Clotting is a common reason that an injection appears to go well while areas of the body stay untreated. A water conditioner, a humectant or a dye does not address clotting.
  170. The correct answer is B (A surfactant, which lowers surface tension so the fluid spreads.): A surfactant lowers the surface tension of the solution so it spreads evenly through small vessels instead of beading on the tissue. Surfactants are added when distribution through fine vessels is a concern. Buffers, dyes and water conditioners each do a different job in the mixture.
  171. The correct answer is A (A dye, which gives the pale tissue a more natural tone.): Dyes restore a more natural colour to pale tissues, which matters when the family will see the face. The dye is chosen for the condition of the body, and the result is checked in the lighting the family will see. A humectant, a buffer or a water conditioner does not add colour to tissue.
  172. The correct answer is D (A water conditioner, which stops minerals interfering with the chemicals.): A water conditioner is added when the water supply is hard, because dissolved minerals interfere with the chemicals and prevent the solution from working as intended. Hard water also affects the cleaning products used in the preparation room. Buffers, humectants and dyes serve other purposes in the mixture.
  173. The correct answer is C (Minerals in the water interfere with the chemicals in the solution.): Dissolved minerals in hard water work against the chemicals, so the solution does not act on the tissue as the licensee intended even when the mixing was correct. Treating the water or using softened water removes that problem before the mixture is made. A stronger solution, faster drying or bacteria do not explain the result.
  174. The correct answer is B (Treat the water or use softened water before mixing the fluid.): Hard water leaves minerals that interfere with the chemicals in the solution, and the mineral film and poor lather are signs that the water needs treatment. Using softened water or adding a water conditioner corrects the problem before the mixture is made. More fluid, heated water or extra dye does not remove the minerals.
  175. The correct answer is A (The concentrated fluid is mixed with water before it is injected.): Primary dilution happens in the mixing container, where the licensee combines the concentrated fluid with water to reach the strength the case needs. The fluid that thins the solution once it is inside the body is a separate idea called secondary dilution. Cavity fluid is not mixed with water in this step.
  176. The correct answer is D (Blood and tissue fluids in the body lower the strength of the solution.): Secondary dilution is the thinning of the arterial solution by the blood and tissue fluids already present in the body. A body that holds excess fluid, such as a case with oedema, dilutes the solution more than a dry body does. Mixing in the tank, drainage and cavity treatment are different steps in the process.
  177. The correct answer is B (Revise the plan upward and record the new information.): New information about the delay and the warm room changes the case analysis, so the plan is revised before the injection begins and the reason is written in the record. Carrying on with a strength chosen without that information leaves the body under-treated. A written statement is not required for the licensee to act on what the family has said.
  178. The correct answer is C (The tissue will have changed more than the short delay alone suggests.): A fever in the days before death means the body was warm and organisms were active, so change can be further along than the short delay would otherwise suggest. The licensee plans for a case that needs more preserving power and closer attention. A short delay does not cancel the effect of the fever.
  179. The correct answer is A (It is an intrinsic factor that helps the licensee expect the body's condition.): The cause of death is an intrinsic factor, and it helps the licensee anticipate how the body may have changed before the preparation begins. The volume of solution is estimated from the weight of the body, while the pressure and the vessel are chosen from the condition of the vessels and the areas that need treatment. Inspecting the body still comes before the plan is finished.
  180. The correct answer is D (The person had a fever during the last day of life.): Intrinsic factors come from within the body and include the cause of death, the medical history, infection, oedema, emaciation and the weight and build of the person. A fever is part of the medical history of the person who died. Refrigeration, room temperature and the delay before preparation come from outside the body.
  181. The correct answer is C (The body waited more than a day in a warm room.): Extrinsic factors come from outside the body and include the time since death, the temperature of the environment, refrigeration, the position of the body, the condition of the skin and the length of viewing planned. A warm room and a long wait are conditions around the body rather than conditions within it. Kidney disease, weight loss and infection are intrinsic factors.
  182. The correct answer is B (The drugs can change how the tissues respond and add a hazard.): Chemotherapy can leave the tissues fragile or dry, and the drugs may still be present in the body, so the licensee plans the technique and the protective measures for that history. The information belongs in the medical history section of the case analysis. Body weight, drainage and the idea that drugs stop mattering at death do not describe how this factor affects the plan.
  183. The correct answer is A (The excess fluid held in the tissue weakens the solution from within.): The strength the tissues finally receive depends on both dilutions, and a body with widespread oedema holds a large volume of fluid that thins the arterial solution from within. A stronger mix at the start offsets that loss of strength in the tissues. Extra water, drainage and cavity treatment are separate steps and do not explain the decision.
  184. The correct answer is D (Moderate pressure with a slow rate and close monitoring.): Thin, fragile tissue needs moderate pressure and a slow rate of flow, with the licensee watching the face, the neck and the tissues as the injection proceeds. High pressure does not improve distribution and can damage weakened vessels. A fast rate raises the risk of swelling and poor drainage even when the pressure is low.
  185. The correct answer is B (It calls for a plan that holds the appearance as long as possible.): The length and kind of viewing is an extrinsic factor, and a two day viewing calls for a plan that holds the appearance for the whole period. A body that will be seen briefly can be prepared with a weaker solution, which is the opposite of this case. Restoration follows the preservation steps rather than coming before them.
  186. The correct answer is C (A stronger solution, careful drainage and full cavity treatment.): Three days in a mild room gives the body time to change, so the plan uses a stronger solution, thorough drainage and complete cavity treatment. A weaker mix or a routine plan would leave the body under-preserved. Further storage does not reverse the change that has already taken place.
  187. The correct answer is A (Extra treatment applied to the surface and under the skin.): The signs point to a case that has advanced, so the plan adds surface and hypodermic treatment for areas the arterial solution may not reach, along with thorough cavity treatment. Adding water weakens the solution instead of helping it. The viewing plan and the pressure setting are separate decisions that follow from the condition of the body.
  188. The correct answer is D (The body from the warm area, because heat speeds tissue change.): Warmth speeds the changes that follow death, while refrigeration slows them, so the body from the warm area needs more preserving power after the same delay. The temperature around the body is an extrinsic factor in the case analysis. The delay is equal for the two cases, but the conditions are not.
  189. The correct answer is B (The tissues may hold extra fluid, so drainage needs attention.): Kidney failure with uraemia is linked with waste products and fluid held in the tissues, so the licensee plans for drainage and watches how the tissues respond. The other options describe effects that belong to other conditions. Drainage remains part of the plan in an arterial injection.
  190. The correct answer is C (Choose a fluid made for jaundiced tissue and check the colour.): Jaundice leaves a yellow cast in the tissue that can be difficult to manage, so a fluid formulated for jaundiced tissue is selected and the colour is checked as the injection proceeds. A very strong routine mix, cavity fluid on the skin or no treatment would make the appearance worse instead of better. The planned viewing makes the appearance especially important.
  191. The correct answer is A (Distribution may be slower, so massage and extra time are needed.): Cold tissue slows the movement of the solution, and refrigeration changes the way the fluid distributes during the injection. Gentle massage, extra time and careful monitoring help the fluid reach the tissues. Raising the pressure, expecting less drainage or choosing a weaker solution does not follow from the storage conditions.
  192. The correct answer is D (Plan extra attention to the face, because the colour will be visible.): The position of the body is an extrinsic factor, and pooled blood in the lowest areas can leave colour across the face and the chest. The plan allows extra treatment so the face looks as natural as possible for the family. A weaker solution, no treatment or a closed casket does not follow from the finding.
  193. The correct answer is C (Change comes on quickly in the first case and slowly in the second.): A death from an infectious illness with a fever is a case in which change begins without delay, because organisms are active and the body is warm. A death after a long illness that left the body dry and thin is a case in which change tends to be held back. The way a person died is one of the factors that shapes how the case is approached.
  194. The correct answer is B (Treat the body as possibly infectious and follow routine practices.): A live vaccine contains a living organism, so a body vaccinated shortly before death is handled as a possibly infectious case and the licensee follows routine practices for the whole preparation. Standard arterial embalming and concurrent disinfection protect the licensee, the family and everyone who attends the viewing. Refusing the case, changing only the fluid or skipping disinfection does not match the risk.
  195. The correct answer is A (It is injected before the arterial solution to prepare the vessels.): A pre-injection is given before the arterial solution, and its purpose is to prepare the vascular system so the arterial fluid can distribute. It is used for cases in which clotting or poor circulation is expected. A fluid added to the arterial solution at the same time is a co-injection, which is a different step.
  196. The correct answer is D (A co-injection, which adds a chemical alongside the arterial fluid.): A co-injection is a supplemental chemical added at the same time as the arterial fluid, so both enter the body together. Humectants, dyes and other modifiers are often added this way when the arterial product does not carry enough of them. A pre-injection goes in before the arterial solution, and cavity treatment follows the arterial injection.
  197. The correct answer is C (Start with a pre-injection and then follow with the arterial fluid.): When clotting is expected, a pre-injection is given first to prepare the vascular system so the arterial solution can distribute. Raising the pressure does not clear a clot and can damage the vessels. Cavity treatment follows the arterial injection rather than coming before it.
  198. The correct answer is B (The body weight, because volume is estimated from the mass of the body.): The total amount of arterial solution a routine case needs is estimated from the weight of the body, so the recorded weight is the starting figure. The cause of death and the time since death guide the strength and the technique, and the condition of the skin may call for extra treatment. The volume is then adjusted if the case needs more work than a routine body of that weight.
  199. The correct answer is A (About fifteen litres, because the requirement rises with weight.): The volume is estimated from body weight, so a body of 90 kilograms needs about half again as much solution as a body of 60 kilograms. The proportionate figure here is about 15 litres. The cause of death, the delay and the tissue condition can raise that figure further, but the weight sets the starting point.
  200. The correct answer is D (The weight raises the volume, and the condition sets the strength.): Body weight is used to estimate how much solution the case needs, while the condition of the body guides how strong the solution should be. This body is in good condition with a short viewing, so a routine strength with a larger volume fits the plan. A heavy body does not by itself call for a stronger formaldehyde solution, and the weight is not ignored in the calculation.
  201. The correct answer is B (The right common carotid artery along the side of the neck): The common carotid artery is the usual first choice when the face and the head must be preserved well, because it delivers solution directly to the areas the family will see. Either carotid may be used, and the right side is commonly chosen because blood from the aorta reaches it more directly. The licensee raises the vessel, ties in the tube and watches the distribution before moving on to the rest of the body.
  202. The correct answer is A (The right internal jugular vein beside the carotid artery): The internal jugular vein lies beside the common carotid artery and is the vessel usually opened for drainage when the carotid is used for injection. The embalmer separates the artery and the vein, passes ligatures and opens the vein so blood and fluid leave the head. A drain tube may be inserted, and the flow is watched throughout the injection.
  203. The correct answer is C (The large sternocleidomastoid muscle on the side of the neck): The incision for the common carotid is placed along the border of the sternocleidomastoid muscle, which the licensee can feel before any cut is made. The cut is kept small and follows the linear guide for that vessel. The artery and the vein are then separated and lifted clear of the surrounding tissue.
  204. The correct answer is D (The inguinal ligament and the femoral triangle in the thigh): The femoral artery lies in the femoral triangle, and the inguinal ligament forms the upper boundary of that area. The femoral vein runs beside the artery and is available for drainage when this site is used. The licensee locates the vessel by touch and keeps the incision short and accurate.
  205. The correct answer is C (The axillary artery with the axillary vein beside it): The upper limb can be injected through the axillary artery, the brachial artery or the vessels of the forearm, and the matching vein at that site provides drainage. Choosing a site near the shoulder lets the whole arm be treated when flow from the trunk is blocked. The licensee still checks the hand and the fingers, because the small vessels there may need extra treatment.
  206. The correct answer is B (Toward the heart, so that the fluid follows the arterial route): The arterial tube is inserted toward the heart, which sends the solution into the arterial system in the direction blood normally travels. A second tube may be placed in the opposite direction when the case calls for it. Both tubes are tied in place with ligatures before the machine is started.
  207. The correct answer is A (Pass ligatures under each vessel to tie the tubes in place): Once the artery and the vein have been raised, ligatures are passed beneath them so the tubes can be secured and the vessels closed at the end of the injection. The tissue around the vessels is left as undisturbed as possible so the incision stays small. The vein is opened for drainage, and a drain tube may be inserted before the machine is started.
  208. The correct answer is D (Clear the air from the tubing before the connection is made): Air must not be pushed into the arterial system, so the tubing is purged before the tube is connected to the artery. Air in the line can stop the flow of solution and leave areas untreated. The machine, the fluid, the tubing and the drain are all checked before the machine is started.
  209. The correct answer is B (The drain placed lower than the point of injection): Placing the drainage site lower than the injection site lets gravity assist the movement of blood and fluid out of the body. The position is adjusted within the limits the case allows, and limb position is used to help fluid move toward the drainage point. The licensee still watches the face and the neck for swelling.
  210. The correct answer is C (Those areas did not receive enough of the arterial solution): Tissue that stays soft, doughy and poorly coloured has not taken enough solution, while firm tissue with good colour shows that the fluid reached and fixed that area. The licensee improves distribution and may use hypodermic treatment where the vessels are too small to carry solution. The result is checked again before the body is dressed.
  211. The correct answer is A (The tissues are receiving more solution than they can hold): Swelling of the face, distension of the neck and weeping from the skin are signs that fluid is entering the tissues faster than they can hold it. The licensee slows the rate of flow, lowers the pressure and checks the drainage before carrying on. Tightening the drainage control so that fluid stays in the body would add to the problem.
  212. The correct answer is D (Check the drain for a clot or a blockage and clear it): A sudden stop in drainage usually means that a clot or other material has blocked the drain or the vein. Raising the pressure would push against the blockage and could damage vessels or drive fluid into the tissues. The licensee clears the blockage or changes the drainage site, then continues at a controlled pressure and rate.
  213. The correct answer is C (Massage the limbs and reposition them to aid flow): Gentle massage of the arms and the legs moves solution through the small vessels, and repositioning a limb helps the fluid reach the hands and the feet. The competency profile expects the licensee to use massage or other techniques to complete distribution. Raising the pressure inside small vessels can damage them instead of helping.
  214. The correct answer is A (To hold extra fluid in the tissues and firm them): Closing the drain for periods allows pressure to build gently, so more fluid stays in the tissues and a body that holds excess water or looks wasted can be firmed. The drain is reopened between periods and the body is watched closely because the risk of swelling and leakage rises. The technique needs closer monitoring than free drainage.
  215. The correct answer is D (Open another vein at a different site for the drainage): When drainage is blocked at one site, another vein can be opened so blood and fluid keep leaving the body. Drainage does not have to be taken from the vein that matches the injected artery, because another site may serve the case better. Raising the pressure against a blockage risks swelling and damage to the vessels.
  216. The correct answer is B (Split injection, dividing the solution between the two main sites): Split injection means using two or more injection sites and dividing the solution among them, so each part of the body receives its own supply of fluid. It is chosen when one site cannot carry solution to the whole body, as with a blocked vessel or an interrupted arterial tree. Each site is monitored separately for pressure, drainage and firmness.
  217. The correct answer is C (Both common carotids, both axillary arteries and both femorals): Six point injection uses both common carotid arteries, both axillary arteries and both femoral arteries, so every part of the body can be reached around the cut vessels. It is used when the arterial tree has been interrupted, as in an autopsied body. The flow is balanced between the sites as the injection proceeds.
  218. The correct answer is A (Restricted cervical injection through the common carotid artery in the neck): Restricted cervical injection confines the solution to the head and the neck by working through the common carotid artery, so the face can be treated with a fluid chosen for its condition. This is useful when the head needs different handling from the trunk, as with a deeply coloured or swollen face. The licensee still monitors the tissues and the drainage closely.
  219. The correct answer is D (The tissues feel uniformly firm and the colour is even): Uniform firmness and an even colour show that the fluid has reacted with the tissues across the body. Swelling, weeping or cool areas that stay soft point to over-injection or to areas that were missed. The licensee examines the face, the hands and the feet with particular care because those areas are seen during visitation.
  220. The correct answer is B (Hypodermic injection of a small dose into each area): The small vessels of the fingers, the ears and the nose often carry little arterial solution, so a small dose of fluid is injected directly into the tissue with a needle and syringe. The licensee watches for swelling and discolouration as each area is treated. A second cavity treatment would not reach the skin of the face or the hands.
  221. The correct answer is A (Surface treatment with a pack applied to the area): Surface treatment places a preservative product on the skin or on a wound, and a pack or dressing holds it against the tissue for a period of time. It is used where the surface has been damaged or lost, or where arterial solution does not reach. The product is strong, so the eyes and the skin are protected and the area is watched for discolouration.
  222. The correct answer is C (Through a small incision in the abdomen below the ribs): The trocar is inserted through a small incision in the abdomen so that it can be directed into the chest, the abdomen and the pelvic cavity. The contents are aspirated, and cavity fluid is then injected through the same instrument. The opening is cleaned and closed once the treatment is complete.
  223. The correct answer is D (Fluid, gas and partly digested material from the organs): Aspiration draws out the fluid, the gas and the partly digested material that would otherwise decompose and cause odour and swelling. Removing these contents also makes room for cavity fluid to reach the organs and the walls of the cavities. The cavities are aspirated thoroughly before cavity fluid is injected.
  224. The correct answer is C (It is placed directly into the cavities at full strength): Cavity fluid is a much stronger product than arterial fluid and is placed directly into the chest and the abdominal cavities after aspiration. It treats the organs and the walls of the cavities, which arterial solution does not reach in the same way. The amount used depends on the size of the body and the condition of the organs.
  225. The correct answer is D (The size of the body and the condition of the organs): The amount of cavity fluid depends on the size of the body and the condition of the organs, with more fluid used when change is advanced. The licensee also considers how long the body will be viewed and treats the cavities thoroughly rather than relying on one fixed measure. The amount is recorded so the result can be judged later.
  226. The correct answer is B (Arterial injection first, then cavity treatment, then the suturing): Arterial injection comes first so the solution can distribute through the vascular system, and the cavities are treated once the injection is complete. Suturing and closure come last, after the vessels and the openings have been dealt with. Working in another order means treated areas are opened again and contamination is spread.
  227. The correct answer is A (The wound edges meet without puckering or a leaking gap): A well placed suture brings the edges of the wound together cleanly, without puckering the skin or leaving a gap that can leak during visitation. The vessels are ligated before the incision is closed so fluid does not escape from the tube openings. The licensee checks the closed incision again after the body has been washed and dressed.
  228. The correct answer is D (Pack or diaper the openings so they will not leak): The natural orifices and other openings are packed or covered with a diaper so that leakage does not appear on the clothing during the visitation. The competency profile expects the licensee to diaper or pack orifices as required. The openings are checked once more after the body has been dressed and placed in the casket.
  229. The correct answer is B (Ask a colleague to examine the body with fresh eyes): A colleague who has not worked on the case sees what familiarity hides, and the chapter recommends a fresh check of the face, the hands, the neck and the hair. The check looks for leakage, swelling and anything the family would notice. Any problem found at this point can still be corrected before the visitation begins.
  230. The correct answer is C (Wash and dry the body thoroughly, then arrange the hair): The body is washed and dried and the hair is arranged before dressing, so the skin is clean for the cosmetics and the clothing is not soiled. Preparing the body in this order also lets the licensee check the closed incisions and the packed openings once more. Cosmetics are applied after the skin is clean and dry.
  231. The correct answer is A (Airbrush or spray application of the cosmetic colour evenly): An airbrush or spray gives a thin, even layer of colour that blends the restored area with the surrounding skin. Heavy layers of cream or powder can exaggerate the difference in texture and look artificial in the light the family will see. The colour is checked once the shape is correct and again from the viewing distance.
  232. The correct answer is D (The area is cleaned and disinfected and fully preserved): Restoration on tissue that has not been preserved does not last and can put the work at risk, so the area is cleaned, disinfected and injected first. Once the tissue is stable, the shape is rebuilt and the colour is matched afterwards. The order of these steps is a basic rule of restorative work.
  233. The correct answer is B (Small amounts of tissue builder are injected into the cheek): Tissue builder is injected under the skin to add volume, usually in small amounts with the result checked after each addition. Wax rebuilds surface contour rather than deep volume, and cosmetic alone cannot replace lost tissue. The licensee photographs a complex restoration and records which materials were used.
  234. The correct answer is C (Apply wax in thin layers and mould it to match the area): Wax is built up in thin layers and moulded so the rebuilt surface follows the contours of the surrounding tissue. Working in layers lets the licensee check the shape as it develops instead of correcting a heavy application. Colour is matched only after the shape is correct.
  235. The correct answer is D (The distance the family will use during the viewing): The work is judged from the viewing distance the family will use, because a restoration that looks correct up close can look wrong from several feet away. The colour and the shape are checked together in the light the family will see. Close inspection is still useful while the work is being built.
  236. The correct answer is B (A humectant that holds the moisture in the tissues): A humectant holds moisture in the tissues and helps prevent the dehydration that shows on a very thin body. Emaciated cases also need moisture returned to the skin, which may call for topical treatment. Buffers, dyes and anticoagulants serve other purposes in the solution.
  237. The correct answer is A (A stronger solution, thorough aspiration, odour control and close monitoring for leakage): A body that has changed this much needs a stronger solution, thorough aspiration of the cavities, cavity fluid and active odour control. The two day viewing means the licensee must watch for swelling and leakage and be ready to adjust the plan. The family is told honestly that the viewing will be private and that the body will be watched closely.
  238. The correct answer is D (Preserve the fragile tissue gently, then plan the restoration): Burned tissue is fragile and needs gentle handling, so the licensee preserves what can be preserved before any rebuilding begins. Restoration on untreated tissue does not last and can damage the fragile tissue further. The family is also told what can and cannot be achieved before the viewing is confirmed.
  239. The correct answer is C (Explain what can be restored and what the family can expect): The licensee describes what restoration can achieve and how the result will look, so the family can decide with accurate information. Promising a perfect result is not honest, and refusing the case without exploring the options does not serve the family. The chapter says the family is told what will happen and when if the case is referred.
  240. The correct answer is D (Arrange for an experienced licensee or specialist to assist): Asking for help is a professional act, and the family's interest comes first, so a case that is delayed or transferred is better than one that is damaged. The licensee records what was done before the transfer and speaks with the receiving licensee. The family is told what will happen and when.
  241. The correct answer is A (All treated tissues are placed back inside the cavities): The competency profile expects all treated tissues to be returned to the cavities of the autopsied body in an appropriate and recommended manner. The cavities are aspirated thoroughly and receive strong cavity treatment before the autopsy incision is closed. Pathological waste rules apply to soiled materials, not to organs that belong in the body.
  242. The correct answer is B (Strengthen or replace the sutures and close the incision): The sutures across an autopsy incision are strengthened or replaced so that the incision does not open during the viewing. The cavity is treated before the incision is closed, and the closure is checked after the body has been dressed. Concealing the line with wax does not deal with the risk of the incision opening.
  243. The correct answer is C (Close the incision and arrange the hair to conceal the line): The cranial incision is closed and then concealed by arranging the hair in the way the person wore it. The closure is checked so that it does not show during the viewing. The face, the hands and the feet receive extra attention after an autopsy because those areas may need hypodermic treatment.
  244. The correct answer is A (Place a rod in the bone space and suture the incision): Long bones that have been harvested are often replaced with a rod, and the incision through which the tissue was removed is sutured. The licensee checks the appearance of the visible areas with extra care because the shape of the limb may have changed. A stronger solution may also be needed, since the body may have gone without blood flow for a longer period.
  245. The correct answer is C (The fluid should limit the green colour that formaldehyde can produce): Formaldehyde reacts with the bilirubin held in jaundiced tissue and can turn the skin green, so the licensee chooses a fluid made to limit that change. The colour of the face and the eyes is checked as the injection proceeds. The family is told how the condition of the body may affect the result.
  246. The correct answer is D (A stronger solution, careful aspiration and surface treatment of the skin): A body that has been in water holds extra fluid and needs a stronger solution, careful aspiration of the cavities and surface care for areas where the skin has slipped. Water in the stomach and the air passages is removed during aspiration before cavity fluid is injected. The licensee monitors the tissues for swelling and leakage throughout the work.
  247. The correct answer is A (Use universal precautions plus the extra precautions the disease requires): Cases with an infectious disease call for universal precautions plus any additional precautions the disease requires, and some conditions must be reported to public health authorities. The safety of staff and the community comes first, so the plan is built before the work begins. A stronger solution does not replace the precautions that protect the licensee and the public.
  248. The correct answer is B (Refer the case to a facility with equipment rated for the weight): A very large body needs more fluid, more cavity treatment and equipment rated for the weight, and an establishment that cannot provide that equipment should refer the case. Moving a body beyond the rating of the table and the lift puts staff at risk of serious injury. The family is told what will happen and the transfer is arranged with the receiving facility.
  249. The correct answer is C (Add another injection site so the affected limb receives solution): A narrowed or blocked artery cannot carry solution to the leg, so a second injection site is used for the area the first site cannot reach. Raising the pressure may damage the vessels without improving distribution, and a good result in the trunk says nothing about flow in the leg. The licensee records the second site and the reason for it.
  250. The correct answer is D (Record the solution, the vessels, the treatments and the condition): The licensee records the solution strength and volume, the vessels used, the cavity and surface treatments and the condition of the body. The record guides the next similar case and shows that the family's instruction was followed. Good notes also protect the licensee if a question about the case is raised later.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (Treating every body as potentially infectious and using the same barriers): Universal precautions means applying the same protective measures to every body, whatever the cause of death or the person's history. A death record is not a reliable screening tool, because an infection may be unknown to the physician and the family. Extra barriers follow a risk assessment of the task rather than the name of a diagnosis.
  2. The correct answer is B (The hepatitis B virus, hepatitis C virus and human immunodeficiency virus): These three viruses are the bloodborne pathogens named in the chapter, and they pass through blood and certain body fluids. Tuberculosis and influenza move by the airborne and droplet routes rather than by blood. Foodborne organisms such as norovirus and salmonella are not the focus of an exposure control plan for blood.
  3. The correct answer is A (Gown, mask, eye protection, then gloves last before the task): The gown goes on first, then the mask, then eye protection, and the gloves go on last so they stay clean while the other equipment is adjusted. If the gloves go on first, they must touch the mask, the goggles and the ties of the gown. Each item should be checked for fit before the work starts.
  4. The correct answer is C (Gloves, then eye protection, then gown, then mask last): The gloves are the most contaminated item and come off first, and the mask is removed last because handling it can disturb trapped droplets. Each item is removed with care, and the gown is unfastened so the contaminated side stays inward. Hand hygiene follows immediately after the last item comes off.
  5. The correct answer is D (Guide it straight into the container without recapping the needle by hand): Sharps are placed straight into a rigid, puncture-resistant container, and a needle is never recapped by hand, bent or broken before disposal. Leaving it loose on a tray risks a cut during the cleanup. A full container is replaced rather than filled past its limit.
  6. The correct answer is B (Cleaning removes soil, and disinfecting reduces the organisms left behind): Cleaning removes organic soil, and disinfecting reduces the number of organisms on the surface to the level the product is intended to reach. Soil left on a surface shields organisms and uses up the disinfectant, so the chemical works less well. That is why the room and its equipment are cleaned first and disinfected second, at the labelled dilution and contact time.
  7. The correct answer is A (Sterilization carried out according to the manufacturer's written directions): Sterilization destroys all forms of microbial life, including spores, and it is performed on instruments that have already been cleaned according to the manufacturer's directions. Sanitizing and disinfecting reduce the number of organisms but do not destroy every form of microbial life. Rinsing with water and soaking in arterial fluid are not cleaning or sterilization steps.
  8. The correct answer is D (A supply of running water with a device to prevent back-flow): Section 47 requires a holding room to be equipped with a supply of running water and a device to prevent back-flow of water, and section 48 sets the same requirement for an embalming room. The device stops water that has been used in the preparation from being drawn back into the building supply. A softener, a dedicated tank and a storm sewer connection are not the features the regulation names.
  9. The correct answer is B (The room must be in premises the licence identifies as business premises): Section 48(1) states that an embalming room shall be located in premises that a Funeral Establishment Operator Class 1 licence indicates are business premises of the funeral establishment. Ownership, municipal zoning and distance are not the test the section applies. The registrar must be able to see the room as part of the licensed premises.
  10. The correct answer is C (Mechanical ventilation discharging directly to the outside of the building): Section 47 requires a holding room to have mechanical ventilation directly to the outside of the building, and section 48 repeats that requirement for an embalming room. Refrigeration, an eyewash station and a floor drain may be useful, but they are not items on the list the regulation sets for a holding room. Ventilation is what carries vapours and airborne particles out of the room.
  11. The correct answer is A (A Funeral Director Class 1 acting for a licensed Class 1 operator): Section 52 allows an operator to engage only a person who holds a Funeral Director Class 1 licence and is acting on behalf of a Funeral Establishment Operator Class 1 or a Transfer Service Operator Class 1, or a legally qualified medical practitioner.
